This proves membership in $W^{-4}_{-\varepsilon,b}$, which is degree one of <a href="#weighted:current-complex" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="weighted:current-complex">[weighted:current-complex]</a>. The constants of individual currents enter their norms, not the choice of spaces or the dimension of their common cohomology group. ◻

</details>

In particular, if an order-zero $(2,1)$-current $C$ with coefficients in $F\in\{\mathcal O,E\}$ is bounded above in height and satisfies $\mathop{\mathrm{Mass}}(C;Y_l)\le C_Ce^{\mu l}$, then for any $\varepsilon>0$ and $b>\max\{\mu,\varepsilon\}$ the theorem places it in $$W^{-4}_{-\varepsilon,b}(\Lambda^{2,1}\otimes F).$$ The inequality $b>\mu$ makes the weighted mass sum converge. The base Sobolev order remains $-3$ and the degree-one order remains $-4$, independently of both the current and its mass exponent. We will always fix the rates before comparing current classes; no dimension bound uniform over changing weights is needed.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Residue independence

If a linear combination of our currents is exact, its primitive vanishes first on a positive tail and then off the supporting curves by analytic continuation. A local distribution supported on a curve cannot have the required order-zero residue as its Dolbeault derivative. This gives the independence statement that we will use both for scalar and for bundle-valued differentials.

<a id="stmt-curve-residue"></a>
<div id="curve:residue" class="proposition">

**Proposition 23** (Residue obstruction). *Fix $F\in\{\mathcal O,E\}$ and rates $(-\varepsilon,b)$ as in Theorem <a href="#weighted:currents" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:currents">22</a>. For distinct closed irreducible curves $B_i\subset Y$ bounded above in height, let $\eta_i$ be nonzero holomorphic differentials on their normalizations with coefficients in $f_i^*F$. Suppose their pushforward currents have negative-end mass exponents strictly smaller than $b$. Then the classes $$[C_{\eta_i}]\in H^{2,1}_{-\varepsilon,b}(Y,F)$$ are linearly independent: every finite subfamily is independent. For $F=\mathcal O$, even one such differential is impossible.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-curve-residue"><summary>Proof of stmt-curve-residue</summary>

*Proof.* The mass criterion in Theorem <a href="#weighted:currents" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:currents">22</a> puts the currents in the indicated complex. Suppose a finite linear combination is exact: $$\sum_{i=1}^r a_i C_{\eta_i}=\bar\partial_F\beta.$$ The sum is zero on a positive tail. The primitive assertion in Theorem <a href="#weighted:currents" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:currents">22</a> says that $\beta$ is zero there as well. On $Y\setminus\bigcup_iB_i$, its distributional Dolbeault derivative vanishes. Local elliptic regularity makes it a holomorphic section of $K_Y\otimes F$ on that complement. The complement is connected: a path in the connected real four-manifold can be perturbed off the smooth real-codimension-two strata of a finite union of analytic curves, and off its locally discrete singular strata. Only finitely many charts are needed along the compact path. Analytic continuation from the positive tail therefore gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\beta\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^rB_i.$$

<a id="curve:normal-jets"></a>
Suppose $a_i\ne0$. Choose a smooth point of $B_i$, outside the other curves, where $\eta_i\ne0$. Such a point exists since the singular and intersection sets are locally discrete and a nonzero holomorphic differential has isolated zeros. In holomorphic coordinates $(z,w)$ and a holomorphic frame of $F$, the curve is $w=0$, the differential is $a(z)\,dz$ with $a\not\equiv0$, and $\beta=u\,dz\wedge dw$ for a distribution supported on $w=0$. Its local finite normal-jet expansion has the form $$\label{curve:normal-jets}
 u=\sum_{p+q\le N}u_{pq}(z)\,
       \partial_w^p\partial_{\bar w}^q\delta_0(w),$$ with distributional tangential coefficients. This is the support theorem for distributions <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Hormander1990](#ref-Hormander1990), Theorem 2.3.5]; locally it also follows by taking a finite-order bound for $u$, Taylor-expanding a test function in its normal variables, and cutting off in $|w|<\delta$. A test function whose normal derivatives through order $N$ vanish on $w=0$ has $C^N$ norm $O(\delta)$ after that cutoff, and hence pairs to zero as $\delta\to0$. Thus only the displayed finite Taylor jet can matter. The normal jets in <a href="#curve:normal-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:normal-jets">[curve:normal-jets]</a> are independent, as is seen by prescribing those jets of test functions.

The coefficient of the normal exterior-form direction in $\bar\partial\beta$ is $\partial_{\bar w}u$; its tangential direction cannot contribute to that coefficient. The corresponding coefficient of the right-hand side is a nonzero constant multiple of $a_i a(z)\delta_0(w)$. But normal differentiation in <a href="#curve:normal-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:normal-jets">[curve:normal-jets]</a> increases $q$ by one. Comparing the largest $q+1$, then descending, forces every $u_{pq}$ to vanish and leaves zero in place of this nonzero order-zero delta mass. This contradiction proves independence. Notice that the familiar principal-value meromorphic primitive of a residue is not supported on the divisor; the support conclusion above is essential.

For $F=\mathcal O$, Theorem <a href="#weighted:currents" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:currents">22</a> makes every closed current of the kind under consideration exact: its support is bounded above, so a single linear weight $(-b,b)$ supplies the scalar primitive. The independence assertion then rules out even one nonzero current. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### The topology of the normalization

An $L^2$ differential satisfies the required current mass bound by Cauchy–Schwarz and the area estimate. Thus the scalar residue obstruction excludes such differentials. The next proposition turns this analytic conclusion into a restriction on the topology of the normalization, including when it is noncompact.

<a id="stmt-curve-genus"></a>
<div id="curve:genus" class="proposition">

**Proposition 24** (Genus zero). *The normalization $R$ of any component of $D$ has no nonzero square-integrable scalar holomorphic differential and has genus zero. If $R$ is compact, it is biholomorphic to $\mathbb P^1$. If it is noncompact, it is biholomorphic to a domain in $\mathbb P^1$, without a restriction on the connectivity of that domain.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-curve-genus"><summary>Proof of stmt-curve-genus</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\eta$ be a square-integrable holomorphic differential on $R$. Cauchy–Schwarz in <a href="#curve:mass-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:mass-estimate">[curve:mass-estimate]</a> gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{Mass}}(C_\eta;Y_l)
 \le c\|\eta\|_{L^2(R)}
       \left(\int_{f^{-1}(Y_l)}|df|^2dA_R\right)^{1/2}
 \le C\|\eta\|_{L^2(R)}e^{Al/2}.$$ Here the energy of the holomorphic normalization is a fixed multiple of the reduced curve area; ramification and singular points are measure-zero sets. Lemma <a href="#curve:area" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curve:area">21</a> gives the last bound. The scalar case of Proposition <a href="#curve:residue" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curve:residue">23</a> forces $\eta=0$.

We give the topological consequence also for open $R$. A handle in an oriented surface contains two embedded circles with algebraic intersection one. Smooth closed forms $\alpha,\beta$, supported in small annular neighborhoods of these circles and dual to them, have $$\int_R\alpha\wedge\beta=1.$$ For example a bump function of the transverse coordinate, times its differential and with integral one, gives each such dual form. Choose any smooth conformal metric and let $V$ be the closure of $dC_c^\infty(R,\mathbb R)$ in the real Hilbert space of $L^2$ one-forms. Set $u=\alpha-\operatorname{proj}_V\alpha$. Forms in $V$ are weakly closed, so $du=0$; orthogonality to $dC_c^\infty$ gives $d^*u=0$. Integration by parts against the compactly supported closed form $\beta$, followed by $L^2$ continuity, gives $$\int_Ru\wedge\beta=\int_R\alpha\wedge\beta=1.$$ Thus $u\ne0$. Local elliptic regularity makes $u$ smooth. With the complex orientation, $u+i*u$ is a nonzero $(1,0)$-form; the two equations for $u$ make it closed and hence holomorphic. It is $L^2$, a contradiction. No closed-range assertion for the de Rham differential on a noncompact surface has been used.

There is consequently no handle in any compact subsurface of $R$. This is genus zero. Equivalently, every Jordan curve separates: a nonseparating Jordan curve can be joined from one side to the other in its complement, producing a second circle with intersection one and hence a handle in a compact neighborhood. A compact genus-zero Riemann surface is $\mathbb P^1$. For noncompact $R$, apply Koebe’s general planar uniformization theorem in the form <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Hemasundar2011](#ref-Hemasundar2011), Theorem 1.1]: every planar Riemann surface admits a conformal embedding into the Riemann sphere. The criterion that every Jordan curve separates is its planarity hypothesis. The theorem allows arbitrary connectivity, so no finite-type assumption on $R$ is being added. Its image is open, and thus is the asserted domain. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:cylinder"></a>
### A cylinder estimate and compactness of the components

The normalizations of the divisor components have genus zero by Proposition <a href="#curve:genus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curve:genus">24</a>. We prove a cylinder estimate and use it twice to exclude noncompact normalizations. First, on a planar domain omitting at least two points of the sphere, the estimate controls a nonzero scalar differential, contradicting the scalar residue obstruction. The only remaining open normalization is the plane. There a periodic gauge lets us apply the same estimate to bundle-valued differentials on infinitely many translated curves, contradicting finite-dimensionality of their fixed cohomology space.

The estimate controls the integral of a differential over a specified target height slab by the curve’s area in a fixed enlargement of that slab. Its constants must remain bounded along arbitrarily long chains of low-energy source cylinders.

<a id="cylinder:area-input"></a>
We retain the exponent $A>0$ of Lemma <a href="#curve:area" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curve:area">21</a>. It was fixed from the coordinate geometry before the holomorphic structure on $E$ was chosen. Its correction exponent satisfies $\gamma>2A+10$, as prescribed in <a href="#curve:gamma-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:gamma-choice">[curve:gamma-choice]</a>. The normalization of any component of $D$, and of any fixed deck translate of that component, satisfies $$\label{cylinder:area-input}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Area}}\bigl(B\cap h^{-1}([-\ell-1,-\ell])\bigr)
       \leq C_B e^{A\ell}\qquad(\ell\in\mathbb N).$$ The constant $C_B$ may depend on the translate. The exponent does not.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Estimates on source cylinders

<a id="cylinder:energy"></a>
Write $\mathcal C=\mathbb R\times(\mathbb R/2\pi\mathbb Z)$, with coordinate $v=t+i\theta$ and flat area $dA_v=dt\,d\theta$. Put $$S_j=[j,j+1]\times(\mathbb R/2\pi\mathbb Z),\qquad
 C_j^r=(j-r,j+1+r)\times(\mathbb R/2\pi\mathbb Z).$$ Strip boundaries have zero area and can be included when specifying a point that a strip contains. For a holomorphic map $f$ into $Y$ let $u(v)=|df_v(\partial_t)|$. Conformality gives $$\label{cylinder:energy}
 f^*\omega=u^2dA_v,$$ where $\omega$ is the Hermitian area form on complex tangent lines. Thus $u$ differs from the Hilbert–Schmidt norm $|df|$ only by the fixed factor $\sqrt2$. We use $u$ in the estimates to avoid this factor.

A finite nested coordinate cover of $X$, lifted to $Y$ and recentered inside its smaller members, gives a radius $r>0$ and centered holomorphic coordinate balls $U_p(4r)$ about every $p\in Y$. Fix these charts so that the Euclidean and lifted tangent norms are comparable with one constant $K\geq1$. Decreasing $r$ once if necessary makes all the recentered balls lie in the larger original charts. The smaller concentric balls $U_p(r)$ will be used in the definition of a flat strip.

<a id="stmt-cylinder-small-energy"></a>
<div id="cylinder:small-energy" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Small energy fills a source cylinder). *There are an integer $P\geq2$ and positive constants $Q,\delta,\varepsilon_0,D_0$, depending only on the lifted geometry, with the following property. Suppose that $\Omega\subset\mathcal C$ is a domain and $f:\Omega\to Y$ is a nonconstant proper holomorphic map. A strip $S_j$ is called *flat* if $\overline{C_j^{2P+2}}\subset\Omega$ and the image of this entire thickened cylinder under $f$ is contained in $U_p(r)$ for one of the fixed coordinate charts. Every flat strip satisfies $\sup_{S_j}u\leq D_0$.*

<a id="cylinder:energy-cost"></a>
*For every interval $I\subset\mathbb R$, if $S_j$ contains a point $v_j$ with $h(f(v_j))\in I$ and $S_j$ is not flat, then $$\label{cylinder:energy-cost}
 \int_{\Omega\cap C_j^Q\cap(h\circ f)^{-1}(I+[-\delta,\delta])}
              u^2\,dA_v\geq\varepsilon_0.$$ The same assertion holds on a source half-cylinder whenever $\overline{C_j^Q}$ lies in its interior.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cylinder-small-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-cylinder-small-energy</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $\delta$ strictly larger than twice the maximum height oscillation of the balls $U_p(4r)$, with a fixed positive margin; a uniform multiple of $8Kr\|dh\|_\infty+1$ suffices after increasing the chart comparison constant.

Choose $P\geq2$ large enough that <a id="cylinder:q"></a>$$\label{cylinder:q}
 q:=\frac{2K^2}{e^P-1}\leq\frac12,$$ and set $Q=3P+4$. Cauchy’s estimate on source disks of radius one, inside $C_j^{2P+2}$, gives a uniform bound $D_0$ for $u$ on $\overline{C_j^{2P}}$ whenever $S_j$ is flat. The norm of a coordinate map with image in $U_p(r)$ is at most $r$, so this bound depends only on $K,r$, and the fixed disk radius. In particular it bounds $u$ on $S_j$.

It remains to prove a uniform positive energy cost. The issue is that the source domain may omit part of the thickened cylinder. We show that a sequence with energy tending to zero has a horizontal graph limit; proper projection near that limit then forces the missing source points to be present. If no $\varepsilon_0$ works, there are maps $f_\nu$, nonflat strips, and marked points as in the statement for which the integral in <a href="#cylinder:energy-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:energy-cost">[cylinder:energy-cost]</a> tends to zero. Translate source indices to $j=0$ and translate the targets and their height intervals by the same powers of $T$ so that the marked images lie in a fixed compact set. After a subsequence, the marked source points tend to $v_\infty\in S_0$ and their images tend to $p\in Y$. Denote the corresponding translated intervals by $I_\nu$; their lengths play no role in what follows. Work in the fixed coordinate ball $U_p(4r)$. For large $\nu$, the marked image is arbitrarily close to $p$, so the strict margin in $\delta$ makes every point of this ball have height in the corresponding $I_\nu+[-\delta,\delta]$, even when the marked height lies at an endpoint of $I_\nu$. The energy of the graph portions in $C_0^Q\times U_p(4r)$ therefore tends to zero in the target direction.

These portions $$\Gamma_\nu=\{(v,f_\nu(v)):v\in\Omega_\nu\cap C_0^Q,
                                  \ f_\nu(v)\in U_p(4r)\}$$ are closed analytic curves in that product. To check closedness, suppose $(v_k,f_\nu(v_k))$ converges in a compact subset of the product. Properness of $f_\nu$ puts the $v_k$ in a compact subset of $\Omega_\nu$; their limit is consequently in its domain and belongs to the graph. On a half-cylinder obtained by cutting a proper full normalization, the same proof works on compact source disks away from its artificial finite boundary; properness is applied to the ambient map. This is the place where properness is essential.

The product Hermitian area of a graph is the sum of its horizontal and vertical areas. Its horizontal area is at most $\mathop{\mathrm{Area}}(C_0^Q)$, because the source projection has at most one point in each fiber. Its vertical area tends to zero. Bishop’s compactness theorem <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[Bishop1964](#ref-Bishop1964)], in the analytic-cycle and support formulation of <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[HarveyShiffman1974](#ref-HarveyShiffman1974), Theorems 3.9–3.10, pp. 585–586], therefore gives a subsequence converging locally as positive analytic cycles, and with convergence of their supports, to a nonempty pure one-dimensional analytic cycle $\Gamma_\infty$ through $(v_\infty,p)$. These theorems apply in local product coordinates (equivalently, identify the finite cylinder with an annulus). The locally bounded graph areas give their mass hypothesis. Convergence of positive currents makes the limit’s vertical area zero. On the regular part of each component this says that the target coordinate functions have zero derivative, so each component is horizontal. Analytic continuation in the connected cylinder then gives $$C_0^Q\times\{p\}\subset\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\Gamma_\infty.$$ For completeness, horizontal components cannot disappear at an interior source point: the defining holomorphic functions of the limit, restricted to the horizontal slice, vanish on an open set and hence on the whole connected slice.

We spell out why convergence to this slice fills a smaller cylinder. Fix a source disk $V\Subset C_0^Q$. Choose a small target ball $B(p,\eta)\Subset U_p(r)$ whose closure meets no other horizontal component of the limit. Such a ball exists by local finiteness of the analytic cycle. On a slightly larger source disk the approximating graphs eventually avoid the target boundary $\partial B(p,\eta)$: otherwise a convergent sequence of graph points would give a limit point on that boundary. Thus the projection of the graph portion inside $V\times B(p,\eta)$ is proper. It is finite, since its fibers have at most one point, and it has nonempty image by convergence to the horizontal slice. A nonconstant finite holomorphic map of analytic curves is open, and a proper map is closed; its image is therefore all of the connected disk $V$. Its degree is exactly one, by the same single-point fiber bound. In particular every point of $V$ belongs to $\Omega_\nu$, and its image is in $B(p,\eta)$. If there were a second horizontal component, the same argument in a disjoint target ball would give two graph points over one source point. Thus there is no second component.

Cover $\overline{C_0^{2P+2}}$ by finitely many such disks. It follows that for all sufficiently large $\nu$ this entire compact cylinder belongs to $\Omega_\nu$ and its image is in $U_p(r)$. Interior Cauchy estimates also give convergence of its coordinate derivatives to zero on smaller cylinders. In particular $S_0$ is flat, contradicting its choice. This proves <a href="#cylinder:energy-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:energy-cost">[cylinder:energy-cost]</a> and all the asserted uniformities. ◻

</details>

Nonflat strips therefore cost a fixed amount of area near any marked height. For the flat strips we use a different bound: along an arbitrarily long consecutive chain, the sum of the derivative suprema remains bounded. This will also control the variation of target height.

<a id="stmt-cylinder-flat-chains"></a>
<div id="cylinder:flat-chains" class="lemma">

<a id="cylinder:chain-sum"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Flat chains). *Let $J\subset\mathbb Z$ be a maximal interval of flat indices for a map as in Lemma <a href="#cylinder:small-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cylinder:small-energy">25</a>. Put $m_j=\sup_{S_j}u$. There is a constant $L_0$, depending only on the fixed geometry, such that $$\label{cylinder:chain-sum}
 \sum_{j\in J}m_j\leq L_0.$$ A doubly infinite chain is impossible for a nonconstant map. If a chain contains a point with height in an interval $I$, every finite endpoint of the chain has a neighboring nonflat strip containing a point with height in $I+[-\Lambda,\Lambda]$, where $\Lambda=\|dh\|_\infty L_0$ is fixed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cylinder-flat-chains"><summary>Proof of stmt-cylinder-flat-chains</summary>

<a id="cylinder:contraction"></a>
*Proof.* Take a flat strip $S_j$, and express $f$ in its coordinate chart as a vector-valued periodic holomorphic function $F(v)$. Write $H=\partial_vF$. Since $F$ is single valued around the cylinder, $$\int_0^{2\pi}H(t+i\theta)\,d\theta
   =\frac1i\int_0^{2\pi}\partial_\theta F(t+i\theta)\,d\theta=0.$$ Consequently the Laurent–Fourier expansion is $H(v)=\sum_{n\ne0}c_ne^{nv}$. Bound the positive coefficients on the circle $t=j+1+P$ and the negative coefficients on $t=j-P$. For $j\leq t\leq j+1$ their geometric sums give $$|H(t+i\theta)|\leq
 \frac{2}{e^P-1}
 \max\left\{\sup_{t=j-P}|H|,\sup_{t=j+1+P}|H|\right\}.$$ The statement is valid for vector values by taking norms in the Fourier coefficient integrals. Comparing the coordinate and target norms yields $$\label{cylinder:contraction}
 m_j\leq q\max_{|k-j|\leq P}m_k$$ whenever all the indices on the right belong to the chain. Each application uses the chart for its own central strip; the numbers $m_k$ are intrinsic, so changing charts causes no further loss.

All $m_j$ in the chain are at most $D_0$. Iterating <a href="#cylinder:contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:contraction">[cylinder:contraction]</a> inside $J$ therefore bounds $m_j$ by $D_0q^{\lfloor d(j)/P\rfloor}$, where $d(j)$ is the integer distance to the nearer finite end of $J$. On a half-infinite interval use its single finite end. At most $2P$ indices have any prescribed value of $\lfloor d(j)/P\rfloor$, so one may take $$L_0=\frac{2PD_0}{1-q}.$$ If $J=\mathbb Z$, the same iteration is possible arbitrarily often at every index and gives $df=0$; analytic continuation contradicts nonconstancy.

Starting at a marked point of the chain, move in the $t$ direction at its fixed angular coordinate toward either finite endpoint. The change of $h\circ f$ is at most $\|dh\|_\infty\sum_{j\in J}m_j\leq\Lambda$. The shared boundary with the neighboring nonflat strip is in the domain because the endpoint flat strip has a full thickening. That boundary point belongs to the neighboring closed strip and has height in $I+[-\Lambda,\Lambda]$. This proves the charging assertion. Figure <a href="#fig:flat-chain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:flat-chain">1</a> illustrates the derivative decay along the chain and the charge to a neighboring nonflat strip. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:flat-chain">

<figcaption>A flat chain in the source cylinder. The absence of a constant angular mode makes derivative bounds decay away from the chain’s ends. Their sum, and hence the variation of target height along the chain, is bounded independently of its length. A chain meeting a target height slab can therefore be charged to a neighboring nonflat strip in a fixed enlarged slab. The horizontal coordinate is the source coordinate $t$, not the target height $h ∘ f$.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-flat-chain"></a>

 \fill[orange!25] (0,0) rectangle (1,1);
 \fill[blue!9] (1,0) rectangle (10,1);
 \fill[orange!25] (10,0) rectangle (11,1);
 \foreach \x in {0,...,11} {\draw[black!55] (\x,0)--(\x,1);}
 \draw[black!55] (0,0) rectangle (11,1);
 \node[anchor=south,font=\footnotesize] at (0.5,1.08) {nonflat};
 \node[anchor=south,font=\footnotesize] at (10.5,1.08) {nonflat};
 \node[fill=white,inner sep=2pt] at (5.5,0.5)
   {consecutive flat source strips};
 \draw[blue!65!black,thick]
   (1.1,2.75) .. controls (2.1,1.55) and (3.4,1.25) .. (5.5,1.22)
   .. controls (7.6,1.25) and (8.9,1.55) .. (9.9,2.75);
 \node[fill=white,inner sep=2pt] at (5.5,2.35)
   {schematic bound for $m_j=\sup_{S_j}u$};
 \draw[->,blue!65!black] (2.4,1.85)--(3.5,1.42);
 \draw[->,blue!65!black] (8.6,1.85)--(7.5,1.42);
 \draw[<->] (1,-0.35)--(10,-0.35);
 \node[anchor=north] at (5.5,-0.48) {chain length may be arbitrarily large};
 \draw[->] (-0.15,-1.2)--(11.3,-1.2)
   node[anchor=west] {$t$};
 \node[anchor=north,font=\footnotesize] at (5.5,-1.28)
   {source coordinate $v=t+i\theta$};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 30 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

The row consists of two outer orange-shaded boxes labeled "nonflat" at the left and right ends, bounding an inner sequence of lavender-shaded rectangular strips separated by dashed vertical lines, labeled above as "consecutive flat source strips". Above the strips, a blue U-shaped curve decays convexly from both ends towards a low minimum in the interior, with two arrows pointing to it labeled "schematic bound for $m_j = \sup_{S_j} u$". Below that, a horizontal axis with tick marks points to the right labeled "$t$", with the subtitle "source coordinate $v = t + i\theta$".
```


<a id="stmt-cylinder-estimate"></a>
<div id="cylinder:estimate" class="proposition">

<a id="cylinder:estimate-formula"></a>
**Proposition 27** (The cylinder estimate on a target height slab). *There are constants $R_0,C_0>0$ depending only on the lifted geometry with the following property. Let $f:\Omega\to Y$ be a nonconstant proper holomorphic map from a domain in $\mathcal C$. Let $w\geq0$ be measurable and suppose $$M_w:=\sup_{j\in\mathbb Z}\|w\|_{L^2(\Omega\cap S_j)}<\infty.$$ For every interval $I$ of length one, set $$E_I=\int_{\Omega\cap(h\circ f)^{-1}(I+[-R_0,R_0])}
                          u^2\,dA_v.$$ If $E_I<\infty$, then $$\label{cylinder:estimate-formula}
 \int_{\Omega\cap(h\circ f)^{-1}(I)}u\,w\,dA_v
            \leq C_0M_w(1+E_I).$$ The same estimate holds on a full source half-cylinder cut from a proper normalization map; properness is used only on thickened strips away from the artificial initial boundary, and a fixed finite initial block of strips is included in the constant. The integral on the left is always restricted to the inverse image of the target interval $I$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cylinder-estimate"><summary>Proof of stmt-cylinder-estimate</summary>

<a id="cylinder:nonflat-count"></a>
*Proof.* Take $R_0=\Lambda+\delta$, with constants from the two preceding lemmas. Let $\mathcal N_I$ be the set of nonflat strips containing a point of height in $I+[-\Lambda,\Lambda]$. The source cylinders $C_j^Q$ have overlap at most $2Q+3$. Summing <a href="#cylinder:energy-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:energy-cost">[cylinder:energy-cost]</a> over any finite subset of $\mathcal N_I$ gives $$\label{cylinder:nonflat-count}
 \#\mathcal N_I\leq(2Q+3)\varepsilon_0^{-1}E_I.$$ This also proves that the set being counted is finite. In particular, the set $\mathcal N'_I$ of nonflat strips hitting $I$ has this bound. Cauchy–Schwarz, first on each strip restricted to the target slab and then on the finite sum, gives $$\begin{split}
 &\sum_{j\in\mathcal N'_I}
       \int_{\Omega\cap S_j\cap(h\circ f)^{-1}(I)}u\,w\,dA_v\\
 &\qquad\leq
 M_w(\#\mathcal N'_I)^{1/2}
 \left(\sum_{j\in\mathcal N'_I}
       \int_{\Omega\cap S_j\cap(h\circ f)^{-1}(I)}u^2dA_v\right)^{1/2}
 \leq C M_w E_I.
 \end{split}$$ Only the intervals appearing explicitly in these integrals are used; no estimate of the energy outside the enlarged target interval is being assumed.

On one flat chain, <a href="#cylinder:chain-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:chain-sum">[cylinder:chain-sum]</a> and $\|w\|_{L^1(S_j)}\leq\sqrt{2\pi}M_w$ give $$\sum_{j\in J}\int_{S_j}u\,w\,dA_v
      \leq\sqrt{2\pi}M_wL_0.$$ Every chain meeting $I$ has a finite endpoint adjacent to a member of $\mathcal N_I$, by Lemma <a href="#cylinder:flat-chains" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cylinder:flat-chains">26</a>. Each nonflat strip has at most two adjacent chains. Thus at most $2\#\mathcal N_I$ chains contribute, and their integral over the target slab is bounded by their full chain integrals. Combining this bound with <a href="#cylinder:nonflat-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:nonflat-count">[cylinder:nonflat-count]</a> proves <a href="#cylinder:estimate-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:estimate-formula">[cylinder:estimate-formula]</a> on the full cylinder.

For a half-cylinder, omit the fixed finite block of at most $2Q+4$ strips on which the small-energy neighborhoods meet its initial boundary. On this block Cauchy–Schwarz gives $CM_wE_I^{1/2}\leq CM_w(1+E_I)$. Apply the preceding argument to the remaining indices. There is at most one chain reaching the new finite endpoint, and its integral is at most $\sqrt{2\pi}M_wL_0$. All other chains are charged as before. Increasing $C_0$ proves the half-cylinder assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="cylinder:exponential"></a>
For a proper normalization the integral in <a href="#cylinder:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:energy">[cylinder:energy]</a> counts the area of the reduced curve once, away from its discrete singular set. A fixed enlargement of a height interval is covered by a fixed finite number of unit height intervals. Consequently <a href="#cylinder:area-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:area-input">[cylinder:area-input]</a> and Proposition <a href="#cylinder:estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cylinder:estimate">27</a> imply, with the same exponent $A$, $$\label{cylinder:exponential}
 \int_{(h\circ f)^{-1}([-\ell-1,-\ell])}u\,w\,dA_v
          \leq C_{B,w}e^{A\ell}\qquad(\ell\in\mathbb N).$$ On a half-cylinder the integral is also restricted to that end. Finitely many height intervals outside the range where the area estimate was initially proved change only the constant.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Excluding nonplane normalizations

We first apply the estimate with the constant multiplier $w=1$. This supplies a differential with controlled current mass whenever the planar normalization omits at least two points of the sphere.

<a id="stmt-cylinder-plane"></a>
<div id="cylinder:plane" class="corollary">

**Corollary 28**. *If a component of $D$ is noncompact, its normalization is biholomorphic to $\mathbb C$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cylinder-plane"><summary>Proof of stmt-cylinder-plane</summary>

*Proof.* By Proposition <a href="#curve:genus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curve:genus">24</a>, the normalization $R$ is a domain in $\mathbb P^1$. If it omits two distinct points, send them by a Möbius transformation to $0$ and $\infty$. Then $R\subset\mathbb C^*$ is a domain in the logarithmic cylinder and $\alpha=dz/z=dv$ is a globally defined nonzero holomorphic differential on $R$. Formula <a href="#cylinder:exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:exponential">[cylinder:exponential]</a> with $w=1$ bounds the mass of $f_*\alpha$ on every unit height interval by $C_Be^{A\ell}$: pulling back a test one-form of norm at most one costs at most a fixed multiple of $u$, while $|dv|$ is constant. The scalar case of Proposition <a href="#curve:residue" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curve:residue">23</a> forbids this nonzero differential. Thus $\mathbb P^1\setminus R$ has at most one point. It has at least one point because $R$ is noncompact. Removing that single point gives $R\simeq\mathbb C$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Excluding plane normalizations

The scalar residue test leaves only plane normalizations. On a translated plane the differential $dz$ has an exponentially growing coefficient in the logarithmic coordinate. We multiply it by the section of $E$ that tends to zero at the negative end. To control the product, we first solve the pulled-back Dolbeault equation in a periodic gauge whose exponential factors have bounded $L^2$ norms on source strips. The next lemma supplies this gauge, including its angular constant mode. Its input $a$ is an ordinary complex-valued function; in the application it will be the coefficient of the pulled-back connection.

<a id="stmt-cylinder-gauge"></a>
<div id="cylinder:gauge" class="lemma">

<a id="cylinder:gauge-exp"></a>
**Lemma 29** (A periodic gauge on the cylinder). *If $a\in L^1(\mathcal C)\cap L^2(\mathcal C)$, there is a periodic distribution $g$ satisfying $$\tfrac12(\partial_t+i\partial_\theta)g=a,
 \qquad
 \sup_{j\in\mathbb Z}\|g\|_{W^{1,2}(S_j)}
       \leq C\bigl(\|a\|_{L^1}+\|a\|_{L^2}\bigr).$$ For every $p<\infty$ this solution satisfies $$\label{cylinder:gauge-exp}
 \sup_j\int_{S_j}\exp\bigl(p|\operatorname{Re}g|\bigr)dA_v<\infty.$$ If $a$ is smooth in an open set, $g$ is smooth there.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cylinder-gauge"><summary>Proof of stmt-cylinder-gauge</summary>

*Proof.* Let $a_0(t)=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_0^{2\pi}a(t,\theta)d\theta$. Its primitive $$g_0(t)=2\int_{-\infty}^t a_0(s)ds$$ is bounded by $\pi^{-1}\|a\|_{L^1}$, and $g_0'=2a_0\in L^2(\mathbb R)$. This bounds its full $W^{1,2}$ norm on each unit strip, including its mean. For $a_\perp=a-a_0$, take Fourier series in $\theta$ and Fourier transform in $t$. For every integer $n\ne0$ define $$\widehat{g_\perp}(\xi,n)
       =\frac{2\widehat{a_\perp}(\xi,n)}{i\xi-n}.$$ Since $|n|\geq1$, each of the multipliers $1/(i\xi-n)$, $\xi/(i\xi-n)$, and $n/(i\xi-n)$ is bounded in absolute value by one. Plancherel therefore gives $\|g_\perp\|_{W^{1,2}(\mathcal C)}\leq C\|a\|_{L^2}$. The sum $g=g_0+g_\perp$ has the asserted periodicity, equation, and stripwise bound. Local elliptic regularity for $\partial_t+i\partial_\theta$ gives the last assertion.

Let $B$ be a uniform bound for these stripwise norms. The two-dimensional Trudinger inequality on a fixed bounded strip <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Trudinger1967](#ref-Trudinger1967)], including the $L^2$ term in the norm, gives constants $c,C>0$ independent of $j$ such that $$\int_{S_j}\exp\left(\frac{c|\operatorname{Re}g|^2}{B^2}\right)dA_v
       \leq C$$ when $B>0$. One can cut the cylinder at a fixed angle and apply the bounded-rectangle form of that inequality; no boundary value of $g$ is prescribed. The zero-norm case is immediate. For each finite $p$, the inequality $p|x|\leq cx^2/B^2+p^2B^2/(4c)$ proves <a href="#cylinder:gauge-exp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:gauge-exp">[cylinder:gauge-exp]</a>. In particular both $e^{\operatorname{Re}g}$ and $e^{-\operatorname{Re}g}$ have bounded $L^p$ norms on unit strips, for every fixed finite $p$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cylinder-compactness"></a>
<div id="cylinder:compactness" class="theorem">

**Theorem 30**. *Every irreducible component of $D$ is compact and has rational normalization.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cylinder-compactness"><summary>Proof of stmt-cylinder-compactness</summary>

<a id="cylinder:bundle-differential"></a>
*Proof.* It remains, by Proposition <a href="#curve:genus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curve:genus">24</a> and Corollary <a href="#cylinder:plane" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cylinder:plane">28</a>, to exclude a component with proper normalization $f:\mathbb C\to B\subset Y$. Let $M_B=\sup_Bh<\infty$ and $M_D=\sup_Dh<\infty$. The curves $B_k=T^kB$ are distinct, since their height suprema are $M_B+k$. For every sufficiently large positive integer $k$ this supremum exceeds $M_D$, so $B_k$ is not contained in $D$. Write $f_k=T^k\circ f$. We will construct on each of these curves the nonzero $f_k^*E$-valued holomorphic differential $$\label{cylinder:bundle-differential}
 \alpha_k=(f_k^*s)\,dz$$ and prove that its pushforward has growth exponent $A$.

The key point is the cancellation of the growth of $dz$. Properness sends the exterior of the plane to the negative end of $Y$. After a periodic change of frame there, the section coefficient becomes an ordinary holomorphic function tending to zero at infinity, hence of size $O(1/|z|)$. Since $dz=z\,dv$, this leaves only an exponential gauge factor, whose stripwise $L^2$ bound is exactly the multiplier hypothesis in Proposition <a href="#cylinder:estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cylinder:estimate">27</a>.

<a id="cylinder:proper-end"></a>
<a id="cylinder:connection-bound"></a>
Fix one such $k$ temporarily. Properness, together with the upper height bound, implies $$\label{cylinder:proper-end}
 h(f_k(z))\longrightarrow-\infty
                  \quad\hbox{uniformly as }|z|\longrightarrow\infty.$$ Indeed, otherwise a sequence with $|z|\to\infty$ would have image in some compact height slab $\{c\leq h\leq M_B+k\}$, contradicting properness. On an exterior disk $|z|>R_k$ we may therefore use the prescribed smooth negative-end frame of $E$. Choose its Hermitian norm to be one on that end. In this frame write $\bar\partial_E=\bar\partial+\eta$, where $|\eta|\leq C e^{-\gamma|h|}$; this is the choice <a href="#curve:gamma-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:gamma-choice">[curve:gamma-choice]</a>. In the logarithmic coordinate $v=\log z$, write $f_k^*\eta=a(v)d\bar v$. Then $$\label{cylinder:connection-bound}
 |a(v)|\leq C e^{-\gamma|h(f_k(v))|}\,u(v).$$

After increasing $R_k$, the entire image of this exterior disk has negative height. Summing <a href="#cylinder:connection-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:connection-bound">[cylinder:connection-bound]</a> over its target height intervals, using <a href="#cylinder:exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:exponential">[cylinder:exponential]</a> with $w=1$ for the first estimate and <a href="#cylinder:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:energy">[cylinder:energy]</a> for the second, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int |a|\,dA_v
   &\leq C_k\sum_{\ell\geq0}e^{-(\gamma-A)\ell}<\infty,\\
 \int |a|^2\,dA_v
   &\leq C_k\sum_{\ell\geq0}e^{-(2\gamma-A)\ell}<\infty.
\end{aligned}$$ An initial compact annulus only adds a finite quantity. Smoothly cut $a$ off toward the finite side and extend by zero to the full cylinder. This retains both bounds and agrees with $a$ on a slightly smaller exterior disk. Lemma <a href="#cylinder:gauge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cylinder:gauge">29</a> gives a single-valued periodic smooth gauge $g$ there, with $\bar\partial g=a\,d\bar v$ and uniformly bounded stripwise $W^{1,2}$ norms.

<a id="cylinder:H-decay"></a>
Let $\sigma(v)$ be the coefficient of $f_k^*s$ in the same smooth frame. Holomorphicity says $\bar\partial\sigma+a\sigma\,d\bar v=0$. Hence $$H(v)=e^{g(v)}\sigma(v)$$ is ordinary holomorphic and periodic on the exterior cylinder. By Proposition <a href="#compact:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="compact:section">20</a> and <a href="#cylinder:proper-end" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:proper-end">[cylinder:proper-end]</a>, $\sup_{S_j}|\sigma|\to0$ as $j\to\infty$. The exponential estimate <a href="#cylinder:gauge-exp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:gauge-exp">[cylinder:gauge-exp]</a> therefore yields $$\int_{S_j}|H|^2dA_v
 \leq\left(\sup_{S_j}|\sigma|^2\right)
                    \int_{S_j}e^{2\operatorname{Re}g}dA_v
       \longrightarrow0.$$ The mean-value inequality on fixed small disks in the cylinder, each contained in the union of three adjacent strips, implies $H(v)\to0$ uniformly as $t\to\infty$. Viewed as a holomorphic function of $z$, it consequently extends holomorphically across $z=\infty$ with value zero. Its Taylor expansion in $1/z$ gives $$\label{cylinder:H-decay}
 |H(z)|\leq C_k|z|^{-1}$$ on a further exterior disk.

<a id="cylinder:current-growth"></a>
Since $dz=e^v\,dv$, Equations <a href="#cylinder:bundle-differential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:bundle-differential">[cylinder:bundle-differential]</a> and <a href="#cylinder:H-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:H-decay">[cylinder:H-decay]</a> give the coefficient estimate $$|\alpha_k|_v=|e^v\sigma(v)|
        \leq C_k e^{-\operatorname{Re}g(v)}.$$ Here $|\cdot|_v$ is the norm of its coefficient relative to $dv$ and the unit smooth frame. The right-hand factor has uniformly bounded $L^2$ norms on unit strips by <a href="#cylinder:gauge-exp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:gauge-exp">[cylinder:gauge-exp]</a>. Apply <a href="#cylinder:exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:exponential">[cylinder:exponential]</a> with $w=e^{-\operatorname{Re}g}$. Pullback of a test one-form costs at most $Cu$, so this proves $$\label{cylinder:current-growth}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Mass}}\left((f_k)_*\alpha_k\bigm|_{h^{-1}([-\ell-1,-\ell])}\right)
          \leq C_k'e^{A\ell}\qquad(\ell\geq0).$$ The omitted compact disk in the source has compact image and contributes only on finitely many height intervals, which are absorbed in $C_k'$. The section $f_k^*s$ is nonzero because $B_k\not\subset D$, and $dz$ is nowhere zero; thus $\alpha_k$ is a nonzero holomorphic bundle-valued differential.

Finally fix, once and for all, an admissible $\varepsilon>0$ and a number $b>\max\{A,\varepsilon\}$. The currents in <a href="#cylinder:current-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cylinder:current-growth">[cylinder:current-growth]</a> all belong to the same degree-one space $W^{-4}_{-\varepsilon,b}$ of the shifted complex from Theorem <a href="#weighted:currents" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:currents">22</a>; their coefficients take values in the same bundle $E$. Their positive supports are bounded separately, and any finite collection has a common upper support bound. Proposition <a href="#curve:residue" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curve:residue">23</a> therefore makes the classes of every finite collection linearly independent in the single space $H^{2,1}_{-\varepsilon,b}(Y,E)$. That space is finite dimensional by Theorem <a href="#weighted:currents" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="weighted:currents">22</a>. Infinitely many distinct $B_k$ contradict this finite dimension. The constants $C_k'$ may vary; no bound on their supremum has been used. The exponent $A$, the weights, and the Sobolev orders were fixed throughout. This excludes the plane case and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### Counting rational curves and obtaining the shell

We apply the preceding analysis to every datum of Lemma <a href="#compact:classes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="compact:classes">9</a>, using the decay rate fixed after Lemma <a href="#curve:area" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curve:area">21</a>. Proposition <a href="#compact:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="compact:section">20</a> gives a divisor $D_i$ with ordinary class $j(e_i)$. By Theorem <a href="#cylinder:compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cylinder:compactness">30</a>, every irreducible component of $D_i$ is compact and has rational normalization. The divisor as a whole may still have infinitely many components; it is locally finite and bounded above in height. This distinction will be retained in all intersection pairings below.

<a id="stmt-conclusion-lifts"></a>
<div id="conclusion:lifts" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31** (Lifts of rational curves). *Every compact rational curve in $Y$ projects to a rational curve in $X$. For each rational curve $B\subset X$, the compact irreducible curves in $Y$ projecting onto $B$ form one orbit under $T$. In particular, a collection of $r$ rational curves in $X$ gives at most $nr$ orbits of compact lifts under $S=T^n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-conclusion-lifts"><summary>Proof of stmt-conclusion-lifts</summary>

*Proof.* The image under $\pi$ of a compact curve is a compact analytic curve, because the map restricted to that curve is proper and locally finite. It is nonconstant since $\pi$ is a local biholomorphism. If the upstairs normalization is $\mathbb P^1$, the induced nonconstant map to the normalization of the image is a finite map from $\mathbb P^1$ to a compact Riemann surface. Riemann–Hurwitz forces that surface to have genus zero. Thus the image is rational.

Conversely, let $\nu:\mathbb P^1\to B\subset X$ be the normalization of a rational curve. Since $\mathbb P^1$ is simply connected, the map $\nu$ lifts to a holomorphic map $\widetilde\nu:\mathbb P^1\to Y$ after a choice of the image of one point. Its image $\widetilde B$ is compact and irreducible. Over the smooth part of $B$, its projection has degree one, because $\nu$ does. The lifts of this fixed normalization map form one orbit under the deck group, so their images do also.

To see that these are all the compact irreducible curves over $B$, take such a curve $C$ and a point of it over the smooth part of $B$. Choose the lift of $\nu$ through this point. The local biholomorphism property of $\pi$ makes its image and $C$ agree in a nonempty open curve germ. Irreducibility and analytic continuation then make their images equal. This proves the orbit assertion. Splitting the integer deck powers into their $n$ residue classes proves the bound for $S$.

This argument lifts the normalization map, not the singular topological space $B$. In particular it also applies to a nodal rational curve; the connected components of its full inverse image need not be compact. ◻

</details>

One curve downstairs accounts for infinitely many translates upstairs. We therefore count independent compact classes over a ring in which multiplication by $t$ records one deck translation. A single orbit of compact curves will then impose one linear equation over the fraction field of that ring. The intersection pairing will show that fewer curve orbits than independent classes are impossible.

<a id="conclusion:sesquilinearity"></a>
<a id="conclusion:curve-pairing"></a>
Fix $n$, put $S=T^n$, and set $$\Lambda=\mathbb Q[t,t^{-1}],\qquad
 \mathbb F=\mathbb Q(t),\qquad \overline{f(t)}=f(t^{-1}).$$ On compactly supported cohomology, let $$ta=S_!a:=(S^{-1})^*a.$$ This is the convention that translates the support in the positive $S$-direction. It agrees with the pushforward action $C\mapsto S_*C$ on homology under Poincaré duality. Define <a id="conclusion:laurent-pairing"></a>$$\label{conclusion:laurent-pairing}
 P(a,b)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}\langle a,S_!^k b\rangle t^k
 \quad(a,b\in H_c^2(Y;\mathbb Q)).$$ This is a Laurent polynomial: compactly supported representatives intersect only finitely many translates of one another. Invariance of intersection under $S$ and symmetry in degree two give $$\label{conclusion:sesquilinearity}
 P(ta,b)=tP(a,b),\qquad P(a,tb)=t^{-1}P(a,b),\qquad
 P(b,a)=\overline{P(a,b)}.$$ Thus $P$ is linear in its first variable and conjugate-linear in its second, where conjugation means only the involution $t\mapsto t^{-1}$. For a compact oriented curve $C$, define similarly $$\label{conclusion:curve-pairing}
 Q(a,C)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}\left(\int_{S_*^k C}a\right)t^k.$$ This is finite and $\Lambda$-linear in $a$ with the same convention. Its vanishing is equivalent to the vanishing of the ordinary pairing of $a$ with every $S$-translate of $C$.

<a id="stmt-conclusion-pairing-rank"></a>
<div id="conclusion:pairing-rank" class="lemma">

**Lemma 32** (Nonsingularity of the compact classes). *For the classes $e_1,\ldots,e_d$ of Lemma <a href="#compact:classes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="compact:classes">9</a>, the matrix $P(t)=(P(e_i,e_j))_{i,j=1}^d$ has nonzero determinant in $\Lambda$. Their $\Lambda$-span is free of rank $d$, and $P$ is nonsingular on its extension of scalars to $\mathbb F$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-conclusion-pairing-rank"><summary>Proof of stmt-conclusion-pairing-rank</summary>

<a id="conclusion:evaluation-one"></a>
*Proof.* Let $w_i$ be the transferred classes in $X_n$. Periodizing compactly supported closed representatives of $e_i$ and $e_j$, then integrating over one fundamental period, gives $$\label{conclusion:evaluation-one}
 P(e_i,e_j)(1)
 =\sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}\langle e_i,S_!^k e_j\rangle
 =w_i\cdot w_j.$$ For completeness, the periodization is the locally finite sum of all $S_!$-translates of a representative; it descends to a closed form on $X_n$. In the integral of the wedge product of two such sums, change variables by the translate in the first factor. The translated fundamental periods tile $Y$, leaving the displayed sum indexed by the relative translate of the second factor. Compact supports justify every interchange. In the construction of Lemma <a href="#compact:classes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="compact:classes">9</a> the forms are supported inside the same open period, so terms with $k\neq0$ actually vanish; the periodization identity also records the intrinsic pairing convention.

The classes $w_i$ are independent over $\mathbb Q$ and the form on $X_n$ is negative definite. Their Gram matrix is therefore negative definite, in particular invertible. Equation <a href="#conclusion:evaluation-one" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conclusion:evaluation-one">[conclusion:evaluation-one]</a> implies $\det P(1)\neq0$, so $\det P(t)$ is not the zero Laurent polynomial. The matrix is invertible over $\mathbb F$. If $\sum_i f_i(t)e_i=0$ for $f_i\in\Lambda$, pairing with each $e_j$ and using <a href="#conclusion:sesquilinearity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conclusion:sesquilinearity">[conclusion:sesquilinearity]</a> gives $(f_1,\ldots,f_d)P(t)=0$. Invertibility over $\mathbb F$ forces every $f_i=0$. This proves freeness and the asserted nonsingularity. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-conclusion-curves"></a>
<div id="conclusion:curves" class="proposition">

**Proposition 33** (The required number of rational curves). *The surface $X$ contains at least $b_2(X)$ rational curves.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-conclusion-curves"><summary>Proof of stmt-conclusion-curves</summary>

*Proof.* If there are infinitely many, the conclusion is immediate. Otherwise suppose for a contradiction that their number is $r<b_2(X)$, and list all of them. Choose $n$ so large that $$n\bigl(b_2(X)-r\bigr)>C_0.$$ Lemma <a href="#compact:classes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="compact:classes">9</a> then gives $d>nr$ compact classes. By Lemma <a href="#conclusion:lifts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conclusion:lifts">31</a>, choose representatives $C_1,\ldots,C_q$ for all $S$-orbits of compact irreducible lifts of the listed rational curves, where $q\leq nr<d$.

<a id="conclusion:annihilating-equations"></a>
Consider over $\mathbb F$ the $q$ homogeneous linear equations $$\label{conclusion:annihilating-equations}
 \sum_{i=1}^d f_i(t)\,Q(e_i,C_\ell)=0,
 \qquad 1\leq\ell\leq q.$$ There are more unknowns than equations, so a nonzero solution exists. Multiplying all its entries by a common nonzero denominator gives $f_i\in\Lambda$, not all zero. The compactly supported class $$x=\sum_{i=1}^d f_i(t)e_i$$ is nonzero by Lemma <a href="#conclusion:pairing-rank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conclusion:pairing-rank">32</a>; its support is compact because this is a finite Laurent combination. Equations <a href="#conclusion:annihilating-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conclusion:annihilating-equations">[conclusion:annihilating-equations]</a> and <a href="#conclusion:curve-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conclusion:curve-pairing">[conclusion:curve-pairing]</a> imply that $x$ pairs to zero with every compact rational lift and every one of its $S$-translates.

For each $j$, the ordinary class $j(e_j)$ is represented by the divisor $D_j$. Every component of $D_j$ is compact rational by Theorem <a href="#cylinder:compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cylinder:compactness">30</a>, and its image downstairs is rational by Lemma <a href="#conclusion:lifts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conclusion:lifts">31</a>. Hence each component occurs among the curves just annihilated by $x$. If $\alpha$ is a compactly supported closed real two-form representing $x$, the ordinary divisor identity <a href="#compact:divisor-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="compact:divisor-pairing">[compact:divisor-pairing]</a> gives, for every $k\in\mathbb Z$, $$\langle x,S_!^k e_j\rangle
 =\int_{S_*^kD_j}\alpha=0.$$ Indeed local finiteness makes the last integral a finite sum of integrals over compact components meeting $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\alpha$, and each summand is zero by the curve orthogonality. This argument pairs a compact test class with a locally finite divisor; it never pairs two locally finite cycles or sums an infinite Laurent series.

We have proved $P(x,e_j)=0$ for all $j$. By <a href="#conclusion:sesquilinearity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="conclusion:sesquilinearity">[conclusion:sesquilinearity]</a> this says $(f_1,\ldots,f_d)P(t)=0$, contradicting the nonsingularity of $P(t)$ over $\mathbb F$. Therefore $r\geq b_2(X)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-conclusion-upper-bound"></a>
<div id="conclusion:upper-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 34** (Independence of curve classes). *Under <a href="#surface:no-square-zero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="surface:no-square-zero">[surface:no-square-zero]</a>, the real cohomology classes of any finite collection of distinct curves on $X$ are linearly independent. Consequently $X$ has at most $b_2(X)$ curves in total.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-conclusion-upper-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-conclusion-upper-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose a finite collection admits a nonzero real relation. Split its positive and negative coefficients to obtain effective real divisors $A$ and $B$ with disjoint lists of components and $[A]=[B]=v$; one of them may be empty. If $v\neq0$, both are nonempty and negative definiteness gives $v^2<0$. But positivity of local intersections of distinct complex curves gives $$v^2=A\cdot B\geq0,$$ a contradiction. Thus $v=0$. At least one nonempty effective real divisor, say $A=\sum_{i\in I}a_i B_i$ with all $a_i>0$, has zero real class.

To pass from this real relation to an integral one, express the classes $[B_i]$ in an integral basis of $H^2(X;\mathbb Z)/\operatorname{Tors}$. The relation space is the kernel of an integer matrix, hence has a basis over $\mathbb Q$. Its rational points are dense in its real points. Approximate $(a_i)_{i\in I}$ within this kernel by a rational vector with all coordinates still positive, and clear denominators. The result is a nonzero effective integral divisor with zero real class and hence square zero. This contradicts <a href="#surface:no-square-zero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="surface:no-square-zero">[surface:no-square-zero]</a>. There is no relation. Since $\dim H^2(X;\mathbb R)=b_2(X)$, any collection of more than $b_2(X)$ curves would contain such a finite dependent collection. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-conclusion-upper-bound-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-conclusion-upper-bound</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* If $X$ has a nonzero effective square-zero divisor, Proposition <a href="#surface:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="surface:reduction">4</a><span class="upright">(i)</span> already gives the desired shell. In the remaining case, Proposition <a href="#conclusion:curves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conclusion:curves">33</a> and Lemma <a href="#conclusion:upper-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conclusion:upper-bound">34</a> show that $X$ has exactly $b_2(X)$ rational curves. It is compact and minimal, has $b_1(X)=1$, $\kappa(X)=-\infty$, and $b_2(X)>0$ by the original hypotheses. Every hypothesis of the Main Theorem of Dloussky–Oeljeklaus–Toma is therefore satisfied, so Proposition <a href="#surface:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="surface:reduction">4</a><span class="upright">(ii)</span> applies.

The conclusion in <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[DOT2003](#ref-DOT2003), pp. 283–284] is an open neighborhood $U$ of the unit sphere in $\mathbb C^2\setminus\{0\}$ and a biholomorphism $\phi:U\to\Sigma\subset X$ such that $X\setminus\Sigma$ is connected. This is precisely the conclusion required in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Equivalently, if a shell is described by its nonseparating core sphere, choose a sufficiently small round annular collar in the given holomorphic neighborhood. On either side of the core, the collar coordinate pushes the complement of the core onto the complement of the smaller open collar. The latter is therefore connected as well. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
#### Consequences of the shell

The shell now gives the deformation and topological description stated in the introduction, and removes the remaining class VII exception in the aspherical-surface inequality.

<details id="proof-stmt-conclusion-upper-bound-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-conclusion-upper-bound</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:topology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:topology">2</a>.* Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> supplies a global spherical shell. Kato’s deformation theorem <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[Kato1977](#ref-Kato1977), Theorem 1] then gives a one-parameter deformation with each nonzero fiber a modification of a primary Hopf surface; his Corollary 1 gives $\pi_1(X)\cong\mathbb Z$. In complex dimension two, a modification between smooth surfaces factors as a finite succession of point blowups, with possibly infinitely near centers <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[BHPV2004](#ref-BHPV2004)].

After shrinking $\Delta$, the family is a proper smooth submersion. Ehresmann’s theorem identifies its fibers by diffeomorphisms preserving their continuously varying complex orientations. Every primary Hopf surface is diffeomorphic to $S^1\times S^3$ <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[GauduchonOrnea1998](#ref-GauduchonOrnea1998), 1, p. 1109], and hence has second Betti number zero. Each complex point blowup increases the second Betti number by one and replaces the oriented smooth manifold by its connected sum with $\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{\,2}$ <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[BHPV2004](#ref-BHPV2004)]. Since the fibers have second Betti number $b$, there are exactly $b$ blowups, and the asserted oriented smooth type follows. The product orientation can be chosen compatibly because $S^1\times S^3$ admits an orientation-reversing diffeomorphism. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-conclusion-upper-bound-4"><summary>Proof of stmt-conclusion-upper-bound</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:aspherical-geography" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:aspherical-geography">3</a>.* Albanese–Di Cerbo–Lombardi <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[AlbaneseDiCerboLombardi2025](#ref-AlbaneseDiCerboLombardi2025), Theorem 1.4] prove these conclusions except for the possible case of a class $\mathrm{VII}_0^+$ surface violating the Global Spherical Shell conjecture. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> excludes that case. Corollary <a href="#cor:topology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:topology">2</a> also explains the non-asphericity: such a surface has $\pi_1\cong\mathbb Z$ and $b_2>0$, but an aspherical manifold with fundamental group $\mathbb Z$ is homotopy equivalent to $S^1$. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "A flat chain in the source cylinder. The absence of a constant angular mode makes derivative bounds decay away from the chain’s ends. Their sum, and hence the variation of target height along the chain, is bounded independently of its length. A chain meeting a target height slab can therefore be charged to a neighboring nonflat strip in a fixed enlarged slab. The horizontal coordinate is the source coordinate $t$, not the target height $h ∘ f$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:flat-chain",
      "pdf_page": 30,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:flat-chain",
          "exact_quote": "A flat chain in the source cylinder. The absence of a constant\nangular mode makes derivative bounds decay away from the chain's ends.\nTheir sum, and hence the variation of target height along the chain,\nis bounded independently of its lengt",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.84cm,y=0.75cm,>=Stealth,\n  every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n \\fill[orange!25] (0,0) rectangle (1,1);\n \\fill[blue!9] (1,0) rectangle (10,1);\n \\fill[orange!25] (10,0) rectangle (11,1);\n \\foreach \\x in {0,...,11} {\\draw[black!55] (\\x,0)--(\\x,1);}\n \\draw[black!55] (0,0) rectangle (11,1);\n \\node[anchor=south,font=\\footnotesize] at (0.5,1.08) {nonflat};\n \\node[anchor=south,font=\\footnotesize] at (10.5,1.08) {nonflat};\n \\node[fill=white,inner sep=2pt] at (5.5,0.5)\n   {consecutive flat source strips};\n \\draw[blue!65!black,thick]\n   (1.1,2.75) .. controls (2.1,1.55) and (3.4,1.25) .. (5.5,1.22)\n   .. controls (7.6,1.25) and (8.9,1.55) .. (9.9,2.75);\n \\node[fill=white,inner sep=2pt] at (5.5,2.35)\n   {schematic bound for $m_j=\\sup_{S_j}u$};\n \\draw[->,blue!65!black] (2.4,1.85)--(3.5,1.42);\n \\draw[->,blue!65!black] (8.6,1.85)--(7.5,1.42);\n \\draw[<->] (1,-0.35)--(10,-0.35);\n \\node[anchor=north] at (5.5,-0.48) {chain length may be arbitrarily large};\n \\draw[->] (-0.15,-1.2)--(11.3,-1.2)\n   node[anchor=west] {$t$};\n \\node[anchor=north,font=\\footnotesize] at (5.5,-1.28)\n   {source coordinate $v=t+i\\theta$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A horizontal row of adjacent rectangular strips represents a source cylinder domain. The row consists of two outer orange-shaded boxes labeled \"nonflat\" at the left and right ends, bounding an inner sequence of lavender-shaded rectangular strips separated by dashed vertical lines, labeled above as \"consecutive flat source strips\". Above the strips, a blue U-shaped curve decays convexly from both ends towards a low minimum in the interior, with two arrows pointing to it labeled \"schematic bound for $m_j = \\sup_{S_j} u$\". Below the strips, a horizontal double-headed dimension line spans the sequence of flat strips and is labeled \"chain length may be arbitrarily large\". Below that, a horizontal axis with tick marks points to the right labeled \"$t$\", with the subtitle \"source coordinate $v = t + i\\theta$\"."
    }
